1	# 306 — Lua language spec implementation (reference spec)
2	
3	## Status
4	open
5	
6	## Current behavior
7	Lua boxes today run via `drivers/lua.sh`, which spawns a fresh `luajit`
8	process per box invocation, feeds it stdin, and reads stdout. Process
9	spawn cost dominates; each call is its own fresh interpreter. There is
10	no persistent Lua state between calls.
11	
12	## Concept
13	The Lua language spec implements `lang_spec_t` (issue 303) for Lua. It
14	is the first reference implementation of the spec interface and the
15	proof that the in-process FFI model works. It is also the simplest
16	case — no compile step, no out-of-process server.
17	
18	Each worker thread gets its own `lua_State`. States are not shared.
19	Per-thread states need no locking on the call path; the state's
20	internals are only ever touched by the worker that owns it.
21	
22	`langs/lua/spec.c` is built into `langs/lua/spec.so` and loaded by
23	the pool runner via `dlopen` at startup, like any other spec.
24	
25	## Per-worker state
26	
27	Init creates a fresh `lua_State` and configures it:
28	
29	```c
30	void *lua_init(int worker_idx) {
31	    lua_State *L = luaL_newstate();
32	    luaL_openlibs(L);
33	    set_package_path(L);   // libs/, map src/, meta.json src_dirs
34	    return L;
35	}
36	
37	void lua_teardown(void *handle) {
38	    lua_close((lua_State *)handle);
39	}
40	```
41	
42	`set_package_path` writes `package.path` and `package.cpath` to
43	include exactly one directory: the map's own `src/`. Everything the
44	map needs — vendored libs, helper modules, anything — is copied into
45	`src/` by the compile step (issue 222). The compiled map is then a
46	self-contained directory: ship the directory to someone else and it
47	runs without further setup.
48	
49	This is a tightening of the phase 2 path, which also included
50	SoraMech's central `libs/` and any `meta.json src_dirs`. The new rule
51	is: one place to look for functions, the map's own `src/`, full stop.
52	Compile is the step that resolves dependencies into that location.
53	
54	## File-and-function caching
55	
56	A naive `invoke` would `luaL_loadfile` and look up the function on
57	every call. Both are unnecessary work — the file's chunk does not
58	change during a run, and the function reference is stable.
59	
60	The Lua spec keeps a per-handle cache: for each box source file
61	loaded, store the table returned by the file's chunk as a Lua
62	registry key (`LUA_REGISTRYINDEX`). On first invocation:
63	1. `luaL_loadfile(L, file_path)` — loads the chunk.
64	2. `lua_pcall(L, 0, 1, 0)` — runs the chunk, expecting a returned
65	   table.
66	3. Store the table in the registry under a key derived from `file_path`.
67	
68	On subsequent invocations:
69	1. Look up the registry entry by `file_path`.
70	2. Index it by `fn_name` to get the function reference.
71	3. Skip `luaL_loadfile` entirely.
72	
73	The cache is per-`lua_State` and therefore per-worker; no
74	synchronization is needed.
75	
76	## Box file convention
77	
78	A Lua box file returns a table whose keys are the box's exported
79	function names:
80	
81	```lua
82	-- maps/<name>/src/classify.lua
83	local M = {}
84	
85	function M.classify(text)
86	    if text:match("^%d+$") then return "numeric"
87	    elseif text:match("[a-z]") then return "alpha"
88	    else return "other"
89	    end
90	end
91	
92	return M
93	```
94	
95	The box JSON references it by `ref = "classify.lua"`, `fn = "classify"`.
96	The spec loads `classify.lua`, stores its returned table, and indexes
97	into it with `"classify"` to get the function.
98	
99	This is the existing phase 2 convention — kept as-is.
100	
101	## Input marshalling
102	
103	Inputs arrive at `invoke` as `(void *bytes, int size)` pairs. The
104	spec is responsible for converting each input's bytes into the Lua
105	value type the user's function expects. The user's function sees
106	arguments as native Lua values — never a raw byte buffer that the
107	function has to parse itself.
108	
109	Each input has a declared type in the box JSON
110	(`{"name": "count", "type": "number"}`). The graph loader (issue 305)
111	records this type on the `input_decl_t` and passes the type alongside
112	the bytes when calling `invoke`. The Lua spec dispatches on type:
113	
114	| Declared type | Lua spec action                                             |
115	|---------------|-------------------------------------------------------------|
116	| `string`      | `lua_pushlstring(L, bytes, size)`                           |
117	| `number`      | parse with `strtod`, `lua_pushnumber`                       |
118	| `integer`     | parse with `strtoll`, `lua_pushinteger`                     |
119	| `boolean`     | strcmp against `"true"` / `"false"`, `lua_pushboolean`      |
120	| `json`        | call `dkjson.decode` on the bytes, push the resulting table |
121	| `bytes`       | `lua_pushlstring` (raw, may contain nulls)                  |
122	| `any`         | treat as `string` for now                                   |
123	
124	The Lua function then takes typed arguments:
125	
126	```lua
127	function M.add(a, b)            -- both already numbers
128	    return a + b
129	end
130	
131	function M.classify(payload)    -- payload is already a table
132	    return payload.kind
133	end
134	```
135	
136	The user does not write `tonumber` or `dkjson.decode` calls inside
137	their box function. The spec did that work before the function was
138	called.
139	
140	If a parse fails (`json` input that isn't valid JSON, `number` input
141	that isn't a number), the spec returns nonzero and the dispatch layer
142	aborts (per issue 303).
143	
144	## Return marshalling
145	
146	The Lua function returns one or more values. The spec converts the
147	return into bytes for the output slot:
148	
149	| Return shape           | Spec action                                                   |
150	|------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------|
151	| Single string          | `lua_tolstring` → copy into `out_buf`                         |
152	| Single number          | `snprintf("%.17g", ...)` into `out_buf`                       |
153	| Single boolean         | write `"true"` or `"false"`                                   |
154	| Single table           | `dkjson.encode` into `out_buf`                                |
155	| Single nil             | `*out_size = 0` (zero-byte output)                            |
156	| Multiple values        | wrap into a table `{v1, v2, ...}` and `dkjson.encode`         |
157	
158	`nil` is a valid return. The spec writes zero bytes and sets
159	`*out_size = 0`. Downstream consumers receive a zero-byte slot value;
160	how they interpret it depends on their declared input type (typed
161	`number` consumer fails to parse; typed `string` consumer gets an
162	empty string; typed `json` consumer parses zero bytes as `null` via
163	dkjson). We cannot demand that user functions never return nil, so we
164	handle it.
165	
166	Multiple return values are wrapped into a table and JSON-encoded.
167	Phase 2 silently dropped extra returns; phase 3 keeps them — the
168	serialized form is `[v1, v2, ...]`.
169	
170	Output buffer is sized to the box's declared `output_capacity`
171	(issue 305's `box_t`). If the serialized value exceeds that, abort
172	(per the hard-crash policy in issue 303). For variable-size outputs
173	the box declares `output_capacity = 0` and uses the large-value heap
174	(issue 302); the spec writes into that heap and stores the handle in
175	`out_buf`.
176	
177	dkjson is the table serializer in both directions (input parsing
178	above and output serialization here). It is already vendored.
179	
180	## Error handling
181	
182	Every `lua_pcall` is wrapped:
183	```c
184	int rc = lua_pcall(L, n_args, 1, 0);
185	if (rc != LUA_OK) {
186	    fprintf(stderr, "[lua spec] %s:%s — %s\n",
187	            file_path, fn_name, lua_tostring(L, -1));
188	    lua_pop(L, 1);
189	    return 1;  // dispatch layer aborts the program
190	}
191	```
192	
193	Lua errors (`error(...)`, runtime errors, syntax errors during file
194	load) all surface here as nonzero `pcall` returns. The spec prints
195	the box context and the Lua error message to stderr, then returns
196	nonzero. The dispatch layer aborts.
197	
198	There is no try-and-recover. Per issue 303, errors crash the program.
199	
200	## Build
... [truncated; 200-line cap]
```

```
1	# 307 — C language spec implementation
2	
3	## Status
4	open
5	
6	## Current behavior
7	C boxes today run via `drivers/c.sh`, which compiles the source on
8	demand (mtime-based recompile to a binary), then spawns the binary as
9	a subprocess and reads stdout. Process spawn cost dominates; each call
10	is its own freshly-launched executable. The phase 2 driver compiles
11	to a standalone binary, not a `.so`.
12	
13	## Concept
14	The C language spec implements `lang_spec_t` (issue 303) for C. It is
15	the second reference implementation, and the first one that exercises
16	the `compile` callback. After compile, invocation is in-process via
17	`dlopen` / `dlsym` — the user's compiled function is called directly
18	through a function pointer, with no subprocess and no IPC.
19	
20	This is the "Option 1 — LuaJIT FFI" model from
21	`docs/004-ipc-and-threading.md`, applied to C: same-process call,
22	nanosecond-overhead invocation, native-typed access. The only thing
23	the C spec adds over the Lua spec is the compile step.
24	
25	## Per-worker state
26	
27	`init` returns a small struct holding the dlopen-handle cache:
28	
29	```c
30	typedef struct {
31	    void  *so_handles[MAX_BOXES];   // per-file .so handle, indexed by file id
32	    void  *fn_ptrs   [MAX_BOXES];   // per-(file, fn) cached function pointer
33	    int    n_cached;
34	    // (a hash table could replace these arrays if MAX_BOXES grows large)
35	} c_handle_t;
36	
37	void *c_init(int worker_idx);
38	void  c_teardown(void *handle);    // dlclose every cached handle
39	```
40	
41	Each worker thread has its own cache. The same `.so` will end up
42	loaded once per worker, since `dlopen` with the same path on the same
43	process returns a refcounted handle (the underlying code segment is
44	shared across all callers — the per-worker `dlopen` is just a
45	refcount bump after the first).
46	
47	## Compile callback
48	
49	The C spec's `compile` callback receives the box's metadata in
50	addition to the source and output paths, so it knows the function's
51	signature and can generate the wrapper:
52	
53	```c
54	int c_compile(const char *src_path, const char *out_path, const box_t *box);
55	```
56	
57	(This is a small extension to `lang_spec_t` in issue 303 — `compile`
58	gains a `box_t` pointer. Lua's compile is NULL, so unaffected.)
59	
60	The callback:
61	1. Reads `box->inputs`, `box->returns`, `box->headers`, `box->link_libs`,
62	   `box->cflags` from the box metadata.
63	2. Generates a wrapper `.c` file (see "Wrapper generation" below) in
64	   the map's `compiled/bin/` directory next to where the `.so` will
65	   land.
66	3. Forks and execs the system `cc` with:
67	   - `-shared -fPIC -O2 -Wall` (defaults)
68	   - The user's `cflags`, if any
69	   - The wrapper `.c` and the user's `src_path` (if `ref` is set)
70	   - `-o out_path`
71	   - `-l<lib>` for each entry in `box->link_libs`
72	4. Waits for the compiler, returns 0 on success, 1 on failure.
73	
74	If the resulting `.so` is newer than the user's source and the
75	wrapper, compile is skipped (mtime check, same as phase 2).
76	
77	The compiler is `cc` — the system default. `gcc` on most Linux,
78	`clang` on macOS. No SoraMech-specific compiler dependency.
79	
80	### cflags in box JSON
81	The `cflags` field on the box JSON is a string of additional compiler
82	flags (`-O0 -g`, `-mavx2`, etc.). It is set by the user in the
83	inspector — a "Compile flags" text input shown when a C-language box
84	is selected. The editor saves it as part of the box's JSON; reads
85	preserve the field; saves write it back. Nothing overwrites it.
86	
87	## Box file convention: any C function, no boilerplate
88	
89	**The rule, stated as a rule:** a box author writes code as if
90	SoraMech does not exist. The function they write must be a
91	function their non-SoraMech compiler can compile, their
92	non-SoraMech editor can lint, their non-SoraMech test harness
93	can call. No SoraMech-prefixed macros. No SoraMech-shaped
94	return types. No `#include "soramech.h"`. Nothing in the
95	author's source betrays that the function will ever cross
96	SoraMech's wire.
97	
98	SoraMech bends to the language. The language never bends to
99	SoraMech. This is non-negotiable across every spec; it's the
100	reason for the spec interface in the first place.
101	
102	Users do not write a SoraMech-specific C signature. They reference
103	any C function — whether one they wrote themselves, or one from a
104	library (zlib, OpenCV, libcurl, anything they have headers for) —
105	and the C spec generates a thin wrapper that bridges SoraMech's
106	bytes-in / bytes-out ABI to the function's natural typed C
107	signature.
108	
109	### What the user provides
110	Either:
111	- A `.c` file containing the function, referenced by `ref` in the
112	  box JSON; or
113	- A library function from a header, no `.c` file of their own — the
114	  `ref` field is omitted, the function name and headers are
115	  declared in the box JSON, and the library is named in
116	  `link_libs`.
117	
118	### Box JSON for a user-written function
119	
120	```json
121	{
122	  "id": "classify",
123	  "kind": "call",
124	  "lang": "c",
125	  "ref": "classify.c",
126	  "fn": "classify",
127	  "inputs": [
128	    {"name": "text",   "type": "string"},
129	    {"name": "weight", "type": "int"}
130	  ],
131	  "returns": "int"
132	}
133	```
134	
135	The user's `.c` file is naturally written:
136	
137	```c
138	// maps/<name>/src/classify.c
139	int classify(const char *text, int weight) {
140	    if (text[0] >= '0' && text[0] <= '9') return weight * 1;
141	    return weight * 2;
142	}
143	```
144	
145	No header to include, no macro, no `int classify(SM_BOX_ARGS)`.
146	Just a function with a typed signature.
147	
148	### Box JSON for a library function (no user source)
149	
150	```json
151	{
152	  "id": "compress",
153	  "kind": "call",
154	  "lang": "c",
155	  "fn": "compress2",
156	  "headers": ["<zlib.h>"],
157	  "link_libs": ["z"],
158	  "inputs": [
159	    {"name": "src",   "type": "bytes"},
160	    {"name": "level", "type": "int"}
161	  ],
162	  "returns": "bytes"
163	}
164	```
165	
166	The wrapper includes `<zlib.h>`, calls `compress2`, and the linker
167	pulls in `libz` via `-lz`. No user-written `.c` file.
168	
169	### Wrapper generation
170	
171	The compile callback emits a wrapper `.c` for each C box. The
172	wrapper has a fixed entry-point name (`sm_box_entry`) that the spec
173	always `dlsym`s. It declares the user's function (extern), unpacks
174	SoraMech's input bytes into typed C arguments per the box's `inputs`
175	declaration, calls the user's function, and writes the return value
176	into `sm_out_buf` per the box's `returns` declaration.
177	
178	For `classify` above, the generated wrapper is roughly:
179	
180	```c
181	// generated: classify_wrap.c
182	extern int classify(const char *, int);
183	
184	int sm_box_entry(const void **inputs, const int *sizes, int n,
185	                 void *out_buf, int out_cap, int *out_size) {
186	    const char *text   = (const char *)inputs[0];
187	    int         weight = atoi((const char *)inputs[1]);
188	    int         result = classify(text, weight);
189	    *out_size = snprintf(out_buf, out_cap, "%d", result);
190	    return 0;
191	}
192	```
193	
194	The wrapper is mechanical — same template for every box, varying
195	only in the input-marshalling lines and the return-encoding line.
196	The compile step writes it to `compiled/bin/<box-id>_wrap.c` and
197	compiles `<box-id>_wrap.c` plus the user's source (if any) into a
198	single `.so`.
199	
200	This means the C spec implements typed function calls without
... [truncated; 200-line cap]
```

```
1	/* langs/lang-spec.h — public interface for SoraMech language specs.
2	 *
3	 * What it is, in a sentence: the C contract that every language
4	 * spec (Lua, C, Bash, anything user-added) implements. The pool
5	 * runner discovers specs by dlopen()ing langs/<name>/spec.so at
6	 * startup and reading the exported soramech_lang_spec symbol.
7	 *
8	 * Designed in issue 303. The init barrier (issue 301) calls init()
9	 * for every spec on every worker before unblocking task dispatch;
10	 * the dispatch action (issue 304) calls invoke() per task; the
11	 * compile-button pipeline (issue 309) calls compile() per source
12	 * file for languages whose compile pointer is non-NULL.
13	 *
14	 * Errors: a nonzero return from any callback is fatal. The dispatch
15	 * layer prints the error context and aborts. There is no recovery,
16	 * no retry, no per-task failed state. This is intentional.
17	 *
18	 * Issue 312 extends this contract with optional fast-path
19	 * callbacks: `invoke_native` and `invoke_json` are specialised
20	 * variants the dispatch layer prefers when wire classification
21	 * says they apply; `native_to_json` and `json_to_native` are
22	 * bridges used at language boundaries. Specs that don't set them
23	 * fall back to `invoke` for every call.
24	 */
25	
26	#ifndef SORAMECH_LANG_SPEC_H
27	#define SORAMECH_LANG_SPEC_H
28	
29	#include <stddef.h>
30	
31	#ifdef __cplusplus
32	extern "C" {
33	#endif
34	
35	/* {{{ Shared signatures */
36	/* Invoke signature — shared by `invoke`, `invoke_native`, and
37	 * `invoke_json`. The dispatch layer picks which one to call based
38	 * on the wire-format classification in issue 312. */
39	typedef int (*lang_invoke_fn)(void *handle,
40	                              const char *file_path,
41	                              const char *fn_name,
42	                              const void **input_data,
43	                              const int   *input_sizes,
44	                              int          n_inputs,
45	                              void        *out_buf,
46	                              int          out_buf_capacity,
47	                              int         *out_size);
48	
49	/* Wire-format bridge: convert one direction of native ↔ JSON.
50	 * Used when a value crosses a language boundary. Spec sets one
51	 * or both; if NULL, the dispatch layer treats native and JSON as
52	 * identical byte streams (the current uniform-byte default).
53	 *
54	 * `handle` is the worker's per-language handle (the same one
55	 * `invoke` receives) — bridges may need it to reach a runtime-side
56	 * scratch area: Lua reads/writes via the lua_State stack, C may
57	 * keep a typed-pointer table, Bash may need its sub-process. Specs
58	 * that don't need it ignore the argument.
59	 *
60	 * Stack-protocol convention for `native_to_json`: the producer
61	 * leaves its native value on top of its runtime's working store
62	 * (Lua: top of stack); the bridge consumes it. For `json_to_native`:
63	 * the bridge pushes the reconstructed value onto the same store.
64	 * `src` / `dst` carry only the JSON side. */
65	typedef int (*lang_bridge_fn)(void *handle,
66	                              const void *src, int src_size,
67	                              void *dst, int dst_capacity, int *dst_size);
68	/* }}} */
69	
70	/* {{{ lang_spec_t */
71	typedef struct lang_spec {
72	    const char *name;       /* "lua", "bash", "c", "python", ... */
73	    const char *file_ext;   /* ".lua", ".sh", ".c", ".py"        */
74	
75	    /* init: once per worker thread at pool startup. Returns an
76	     * opaque handle stored in the worker context; the dispatch
77	     * layer passes this handle back to invoke() for every call. */
78	    void *(*init)(int worker_idx);
79	
80	    /* teardown: once per worker at pool shutdown. Frees the handle
81	     * and any per-worker resources (e.g. lua_State, dlopen caches,
82	     * persistent socket connections). */
83	    void  (*teardown)(void *handle);
84	
85	    /* compile: optional. Called once per source file at map-load
86	     * (or at editor compile-button time). For languages that don't
87	     * compile (Lua, Bash), leave this NULL. For C, it runs
88	     * gcc -shared -fPIC -o out_path src_path. Returns 0 on
89	     * success, nonzero on failure. */
90	    int   (*compile)(const char *src_path, const char *out_path);
91	
92	    /* invoke: per box call. Inputs are arrays of (bytes, size) pairs,
93	     * already read from slots by the dispatch layer. Returns the
94	     * function's output by writing to out_buf and setting *out_size.
95	     * Returns 0 on success, nonzero (fatal) on error.
96	     *
97	     * This is the canonical entry point; every spec implements it.
98	     * The dispatch layer falls back to it when the specialised
99	     * paths below are not set. */
100	    lang_invoke_fn invoke;
101	
102	    /* invoke_native — optional fast path used when every wire
103	     * adjacent to this box (input and output) stays inside this
104	     * spec's language. Bytes on the wire are this language's
105	     * native serialisation (Lua tables via msgpack, C struct
106	     * memcpy, etc.) and the encode/decode round trip is skipped.
107	     * NULL means the spec hasn't specialised; dispatch falls
108	     * back to `invoke`. Issue 312. */
109	    lang_invoke_fn invoke_native;
110	
111	    /* invoke_json — optional universal-interop path. Bytes on the
112	     * wire are JSON. NULL means the spec hasn't specialised;
113	     * dispatch falls back to `invoke`. Issue 312. */
114	    lang_invoke_fn invoke_json;
115	
116	    /* Bridges between native and JSON wire forms. Used when a
117	     * value crosses a language boundary — the producer writes
118	     * native, the dispatch layer converts via the producer's
119	     * native_to_json, hands the JSON bytes to the consumer's
120	     * json_to_native. NULL means the spec hasn't specialised;
121	     * dispatch treats native and JSON as identical bytes. Issue
122	     * 312. */
123	    lang_bridge_fn native_to_json;
124	    lang_bridge_fn json_to_native;
125	} lang_spec_t;

## Assistant

Both 306 and 307 have init/invoke/teardown/cache done. What's left in each is the **typed marshalling** — converting SoraMech's bytes-in/bytes-out wire to the natural typed shape of each language. But the current `invoke` signature in `langs/lang-spec.h:39` is bytes-only — it doesn't carry the box's declared input/return types, and the `compile` callback at line 90 doesn't take a `box_t *` either, so the spec can't see types to marshal against.

Two complications worth flagging before we start:

1. **307's typed-wrapper plan needs a contract change.** The issue calls this out: "compile gains a `box_t` pointer." That's a cross-cutting edit to `lang-spec.h` and every spec.
2. **306's "rich return types" was deferred *to issue 312*** (same-language wire fast-path) per the existing implementation log. The dispatch layer already routes through `invoke_native` / `invoke_json` based on per-edge same-language classification (`src/012-dispatch.c:572-574`). So the question is whether typed Lua tables/JSON returns should land here in 306 or as part of 312's invoke_json path.

My read: the cleanest cut is to do the **non-controversial parts of both** first — `package.path` tightening + number/bool/nil/table-via-dkjson returns for Lua, and `cflags`/`headers`/`link_libs` from the box JSON for C — and treat the typed-wrapper / box_t-in-invoke work as a separate issue that touches `lang-spec.h`. Does that split match what you had in mind, or did you want to take on the contract change too?

1	/* langs/lua/spec.c — Lua language spec for SoraMech.
2	 *
3	 * Per-worker handle is a `lua_State` with the standard libraries
4	 * opened. Invocation convention: the box's source file is expected
5	 * to return a Lua table (`return M` at the bottom), and the
6	 * fn_name is a key into that table. Inputs arrive as byte arrays;
7	 * each one is pushed as a Lua string. The function's single return
8	 * value is converted to a string (via tostring semantics) and
9	 * memcpy'd into the caller's output buffer.
10	 *
11	 * Designed in issue 306. Implementation choices that matter:
12	 *
13	 *  - One lua_State per worker thread. lua_State is not
14	 *    thread-safe, but per-thread requires no locking.
15	 *  - The state's stack is rewound to its baseline depth at every
16	 *    invoke entry/exit so cross-call leaks don't accumulate.
17	 *  - File caching (luaL_loadfile once, replayed via lua_call) is
18	 *    deferred — this iteration loads the chunk on every invoke.
19	 *    Lands as a follow-on once profiling justifies it.
20	 *  - The chunk's side effects run on every invoke too (since
21	 *    we re-load and re-execute). Box source files should be pure
22	 *    "return M" modules for now.
23	 */
24	
25	#include "lang-spec.h"
26	#include "json.h"
27	
28	#include <lua.h>
29	#include <lauxlib.h>
30	#include <lualib.h>
31	
32	#include <stdio.h>
33	#include <stdlib.h>
34	#include <string.h>
35	
36	/* Registry key for the per-worker file → module-table cache. The
37	 * key is a string in the Lua registry; the value is a Lua table
38	 * keyed by file path. Each entry's value is the module table the
39	 * file's chunk returned.
40	 *
41	 * Caching is per-`lua_State` (i.e. per-worker), which matches the
42	 * thread model: each worker has its own state, never shared. No
43	 * locking needed because only the worker that owns the state ever
44	 * touches the cache. */
45	#define LUA_CACHE_KEY "soramech.module_cache"
46	
47	/* {{{ lua_init() — create a state, open standard libs, seed cache */
48	static void *lua_init(int worker_idx)
49	{
50	    (void)worker_idx;
51	    lua_State *L = luaL_newstate();
52	    if (!L) return NULL;
53	    luaL_openlibs(L);
54	    /* Seed the empty cache table. */
55	    lua_newtable(L);
56	    lua_setfield(L, LUA_REGISTRYINDEX, LUA_CACHE_KEY);
57	    return L;
58	}
59	/* }}} */
60	
61	/* {{{ lua_teardown() */
62	static void lua_teardown(void *handle)
63	{
64	    if (handle) lua_close((lua_State *)handle);
65	}
66	/* }}} */
67	
68	/* {{{ lua_invoke() */
69	static int lua_invoke(void *handle,
70	                      const char  *file_path,
71	                      const char  *fn_name,
72	                      const void **input_data,
73	                      const int   *input_sizes,
74	                      int          n_inputs,
75	                      void        *out_buf,
76	                      int          out_buf_capacity,
77	                      int         *out_size)
78	{
79	    lua_State *L = (lua_State *)handle;
80	    if (!L || !file_path || !fn_name) return -1;
81	
82	    int baseline = lua_gettop(L);
83	
84	    /* Module cache lookup. The cache table lives in the registry
85	     * under LUA_CACHE_KEY; each key is a file path, each value is
86	     * the module table the file's chunk returned. Stack after this
87	     * block: ..., cache, module. */
88	    lua_getfield(L, LUA_REGISTRYINDEX, LUA_CACHE_KEY);
89	    lua_getfield(L, -1, file_path);
90	    if (lua_isnil(L, -1)) {
91	        lua_pop(L, 1);   /* discard nil */
92	        /* Stack: ..., cache */
93	
94	        if (luaL_loadfile(L, file_path) != 0) {
95	            fprintf(stderr, "lua spec: loadfile('%s'): %s\n",
96	                    file_path, lua_tostring(L, -1));
97	            lua_settop(L, baseline);
98	            return -1;
99	        }
100	        if (lua_pcall(L, 0, 1, 0) != 0) {
101	            fprintf(stderr, "lua spec: chunk exec '%s': %s\n",
102	                    file_path, lua_tostring(L, -1));
103	            lua_settop(L, baseline);
104	            return -1;
105	        }
106	        if (!lua_istable(L, -1)) {
107	            fprintf(stderr, "lua spec: '%s' did not return a table\n", file_path);
108	            lua_settop(L, baseline);
109	            return -1;
110	        }
111	        /* Stack: ..., cache, module. Cache the module under
112	         * file_path. */
113	        lua_pushvalue(L, -1);
114	        lua_setfield(L, -3, file_path);
115	    }
116	    /* Stack at this point: ..., cache, module. */
117	
118	    /* Pull the function out of the module table. */
119	    lua_getfield(L, -1, fn_name);
120	    if (!lua_isfunction(L, -1)) {
121	        fprintf(stderr, "lua spec: '%s' is not a function in %s\n",
122	                fn_name, file_path);
123	        lua_settop(L, baseline);

Looking at the actual code, the "non-controversial" scope is narrower than I implied. Here's the real state:

**For Lua 306**, much of what I called deferred is already done in the bridges:
- `lua_native_to_json` at `langs/lua/spec.c:335` already handles tables/numbers/booleans/nil/strings via streaming JSON. The "rich return types" that the implementation log defers to 312 are effectively done via that bridge.
- `lua_invoke` (the bytes-bytes fallback) at line 152 already coerces strings/numbers/booleans/nil via `lua_tolstring`. Nil hits a zero-byte path.
- What's actually missing is **`package.path` tightening** — the spec never calls `set_package_path` at all. The issue says it should point only at the map's `src/`. That's doable without any contract change: derive the directory from `file_path` on each cache miss and update `package.path` then.

**For C 307**, the remaining items all need the contract change:
- `cflags`, `headers`, `link_libs` from the box JSON all require `compile` to know about the `box_t` (currently it only gets `src_path` and `out_path`).
- Variable-size outputs via the large-value heap also touches the dispatch contract.
- The "lazy compile from `.c`" path at line 115 even works without any of this.

So the genuinely non-controversial slice is **just the Lua package.path work**. That's a small change. Everything else in 307 is the contract change we agreed to defer.

My recommendation: do the Lua `package.path` tightening now (a few dozen lines, no contract change, closes one item on 306), update the 306 issue log, then move to 312. Sound right?

1	# 312 — Same-language wire fast path: skip JSON for adjacent same-language boxes
2	
3	## Status
4	open
5	
6	## Current behavior
7	
8	The wire format between boxes is JSON (`docs/004-ipc-and-threading.md`,
9	issue 302's "no type tags" — slot store stores opaque bytes; the
10	language spec encodes/decodes). Every wire pays the JSON encode +
11	decode cost on every value pass, even when the producer and the
12	consumer are the same language and could use a faster
13	language-native serialization.
14	
15	For a Lua → Lua wire passing a 10-element table:
16	1. Producer's spec: `cjson.encode(table)` → string of N bytes
17	2. `slot_push(slot, string, N)`
18	3. `slot_peek(slot, buf, N)`
19	4. Consumer's spec: `cjson.decode(buf)` → Lua table
20	
21	Steps 1 and 4 are the cost. They're language-agnostic and unnecessary
22	when both endpoints share a language.
23	
24	## Intended behavior
25	
26	Each language spec exposes **two invoke paths**:
27	
28	```c
29	typedef struct {
30	    const char *name;
31	    const char *file_ext;
32	
33	    void *(*init)(int worker_idx);
34	    void  (*teardown)(void *handle);
35	    int   (*compile)(...);
36	
37	    // Universal path — JSON bytes in, JSON bytes out
38	    int   (*invoke_json)  (void *handle, /* common args */);
39	
40	    // Fast path — language-native bytes in, language-native bytes out
41	    int   (*invoke_native)(void *handle, /* common args */);
42	
43	    // Bridges used when a value crosses a language boundary
44	    int   (*native_to_json)(const void *src, int src_size,
45	                            void *dst, int dst_capacity, int *dst_size);
46	    int   (*json_to_native)(const void *src, int src_size,
47	                            void *dst, int dst_capacity, int *dst_size);
48	} lang_spec_t;
49	```
50	
51	`invoke_native` is required (every spec implements one). Per-language
52	"native" format:
53	- **Lua**: `lua_dump` for closures, `cjson` or msgpack for tables, raw
54	  bytes for strings/numbers. Spec's choice as long as it's
55	  symmetric with its own decoder.
56	- **C**: raw struct memcpy. The compile-time wrapper (issue 307)
57	  knows the types; serialization is `memcpy(slot_buf, &val, sizeof
58	  val)`.
59	- **Bash**: strings only — Bash has no other native form, so the
60	  fast path is identical to the JSON path. Implement as a thin
61	  alias.
62	
63	`invoke_json` is the universal interop format. Required for any
64	language that participates in cross-language wires. A spec that
65	only ever talks to itself can skip `invoke_json` (compile error if
66	a wire crosses to it from another language).
67	
68	## Compile-time wire classification
69	
70	Every wire gets a `fast_path: true | false` flag set at build time
71	(issue 309 build step):
72	
73	```python
74	for wire in graph.wires:
75	    src_lang = box_at(wire.from).lang
76	    dst_lang = box_at(wire.to).lang
77	    wire.fast_path = (src_lang == dst_lang)
78	```
79	
80	The flag becomes part of the compiled manifest. Runtime never
81	re-evaluates language pairing.
82	
83	## Per-edge format negotiation
84	
85	Classification is **per edge**, not per box. A box always
86	executes its user function in native form. The format on the
87	wire is decided independently for each input edge and each
88	output edge based on the language pair across that edge:
89	
90	```
91	input edge native?   = (producer.lang == this_box.lang)
92	output edge native?  = (this_box.lang == consumer.lang)
93	```
94	
95	So a Lua box sitting in the middle of `[C → Lua → C]` works
96	like this:
97	
98	- Input edge from the C producer: cross-lang, so the slot
99	  carries JSON. On read, the Lua spec calls `json_to_native`
100	  to lift it into a Lua value.
101	- The box's user function runs natively in Lua. It returns a
102	  Lua value.
103	- Output edges to the two C consumers: cross-lang, so the spec
104	  calls `native_to_json` once and pushes JSON bytes to each
105	  consumer's slot.
106	
107	A Lua box in `[Lua → Lua → C]` works differently *along its
108	own edges*:
109	
110	- Input edge from the Lua producer: same-lang, slot carries
111	  native binary. No conversion on read.
112	- User function runs.
113	- Output edges: one is same-lang (push native), the other is
114	  cross-lang (call `native_to_json`, push JSON). The producer
115	  pushes once to each consumer's slot in the right format.
116	
117	This means **same-lang neighbors get the fast path even when
118	some other neighbor is cross-lang**. The classification doesn't
119	collapse on the first cross-language edge it finds; it stays
120	per-edge.
121	
122	### Why per-edge, not per-box
123	
124	The earlier design considered an "all same-lang or fall back
125	to JSON for all" rule (the simpler classification). It's
126	strictly dominated by the per-edge rule:
127	
128	- The dominant case is "a Lua sub-graph occasionally talks to
129	  C." Per-box would force the Lua-to-Lua interior to round-trip
130	  through JSON for every box that has *any* C neighbor.
131	- Per-edge keeps the cost local to the actual cross-lang
132	  boundary. The interior of any same-lang island skips JSON;
133	  only the boundary edges pay.
134	- The implementation cost is identical: same number of bits per
135	  box, just stored per-port/per-edge instead of per-box.
136	
137	### What each box records at graph load
138	
139	For each box:
140	
141	- `input_edge_native[i]` — one bit per input port. True iff the
142	  producer feeding that port has the same language as this box.
143	- `output_edge_native[j]` — one bit per outgoing connection
144	  (not per output port — a single output port can fan to
145	  multiple consumers, each of which is classified independently
146	  because the language pair may differ across consumers).
147	
148	Both arrays are computed during `graph_attach_runtime` and live
149	on the box record. No runtime re-evaluation.
150	
151	## Runtime dispatch
152	
153	The dispatch action calls `invoke_native` always (when the spec
154	provides one). The boundary work is per-edge in two places:
155	input-read and output-push.
156	
157	### Input-read: per-input-port
158	
159	```c
160	for (int i = 0; i < n_inputs; i++) {
161	    slot_pop(input_slots[i], wire_buf, wire_buf_cap);
162	    if (b->input_edge_native[i]) {
163	        // wire bytes are native form already; hand to invoke_native as-is
164	        native_inputs[i] = wire_buf;
165	    } else {
166	        // wire bytes are JSON; lift into native form
167	        spec->json_to_native(ctx, wire_buf, wire_size,
168	                             native_buf, native_cap, &native_size);
169	        native_inputs[i] = native_buf;
170	    }
171	}
172	spec->invoke_native(ctx, fn, native_inputs, n_inputs,
173	                    native_out, native_out_cap, &native_out_size);
174	```
175	
176	### Output-push: per-outgoing-connection
177	
178	```c
179	for (int j = 0; j < b->n_connections; j++) {
180	    if (b->output_edge_native[j]) {
181	        // consumer is same-lang; push native bytes directly
182	        slot_push(consumer_slot, native_out, native_out_size, tag);
183	    } else {
184	        // consumer is cross-lang; serialize to JSON once per such edge
185	        spec->native_to_json(ctx, native_out, native_out_size,
186	                             json_buf, json_buf_cap, &json_size);
187	        slot_push(consumer_slot, json_buf, json_size, tag);
188	    }
189	}
190	```
191	
192	If multiple cross-lang consumers share the producer's language,
193	the dispatch can cache the JSON encoding from the first such
194	edge and reuse it for the rest (optimization, not required for
195	correctness).
196	
197	If the spec's `invoke_native` is NULL (e.g., bash), the dispatch
198	falls back to the universal `invoke` (JSON-in, JSON-out) and
199	treats every edge as JSON. This is the slow path; it's always
200	correct.
... [truncated; 200-line cap]

Read 312. The hard stop the issue calls out — "ask the user about type-annotated wire encodings" — looks already answered in our memory: **JSON always, with `$ref` / `$function_pointer` / `$lang_opaque` primitives for richer payloads**. That collapses the design: `invoke_native` isn't a different serialization, it's the same JSON wire but allowed to carry the richer sentinels when both endpoints share a language.

Concrete state of 312 right now:

- **Spec interface**: the four new callbacks exist on `lang_spec_t`. ✓
- **Per-box `use_native_invoke` bit**: computed by `graph_attach_runtime`, consulted by `do_call_box` at `src/012-dispatch.c:572`. This is the approximation that needs to become per-edge.
- **Lua bridges**: `native_to_json` and `json_to_native` at `langs/lua/spec.c:335` and `:437` are real (full JSON encode/decode, including the `n`-field array convention). The issue text saying they "all alias to invoke" is **stale** — Lua's are real, but C and Bash are not.
- **`invoke_native` / `invoke_json` proper**: nobody has them yet. Dispatch falls through to `invoke` everywhere.
- **Richer primitives** (`$ref`, `$function_pointer`, `$lang_opaque`): not implemented in any bridge. The Lua encoder hard-errors on function/userdata at `spec.c:317` — those are exactly the values these primitives are supposed to carry.

Three slices, ordered by independence:

1. **Per-edge classification.** Mechanical, unblocked, no design decisions. Split the single `use_native_invoke` bit into the `input_edge_native[i]` / `output_edge_native[j]` arrays the issue describes. Compute them in `graph_attach_runtime`. Branch dispatch's input-read and output-push on the relevant array. The fields are already declared at `010-graph-loader.h:145-146` but unused.

2. **C and Bash bridges.** Write real `native_to_json` / `json_to_native` for both (currently both fall through to bytes-bytes `invoke`). Without these, any cross-language wire involving C or Bash is implicitly broken — the dispatch picks `invoke` and treats whatever bytes are in the slot as both native and JSON.

3. **Richer primitives in the JSON wire.** Define what the three sentinels look like on the wire, how producer and consumer recognize them, and what happens when a `$function_pointer` reaches a cross-language consumer. This one needs design before code.

My recommendation: do (1) first — it's pure plumbing and the issue's implementation status names it as the top item. Then (2). Hold (3) until we hit a concrete need for it. Want me to start on (1)?

● Read 312. The hard stop the issue calls out — "ask the user about type-annotated wire encodings" — looks already answered in our memory:

I don't think this is true, and we'll have to update the documentation to reflect the correct design.

basically, we want json to only be used during cross-language borders. If a wire crosses to a new language, we'll need to convert to json. If it doesn't, we can just pass it along according to how the language spec wants to receive it. The language spec is basically a glorified getter/setter anyway, for the inputs/return values respectively...

you should be able to find more information in the section about compiling the map into a single binary - alternatively, into a directory with all the files for each language concatenated together - all the C files go into the same C file, all the Lua files go into the same Lua file, etc. Then, it's executed by the C graph loader thread pool system

1	# 313 — Research: whole-program same-language compilation
2	
3	## Status
4	research / low priority / blocked until rest of phase 3 is complete
5	
6	## Concept
7	
8	Instead of one box = one function call going through the dispatch
9	layer + slot store + spec invocation, **merge all same-language
10	boxes at compile time into a single translation unit** for that
11	language. Box-to-box transitions within a language region become
12	ordinary function calls — the optimizer can inline across them, the
13	language runtime stays warm, and the slot store and dispatch layer
14	don't run for those transitions at all. Only cross-language wires
15	retain the slot machinery.
16	
17	For C: every C box becomes a function in one big `.c` file, with
18	the build emitting one `.so`. Wire endpoints become direct calls;
19	the optimizer inlines based on its usual heuristics.
20	
21	For Lua: every Lua function gets pre-compiled into one bytecode
22	chunk loaded into one shared `lua_State` per worker. Wire
23	endpoints become direct `lua_pcall`s into that chunk — no JSON
24	encode/decode, no slot push/pop, no spec re-entry per call.
25	
26	For Bash: probably no benefit — Bash is already process-per-call;
27	fusing scripts doesn't help much. Skip.
28	
29	## Why this is research, not a feature
30	
31	Three open questions that need code-level investigation before
32	the design is concrete:
33	
34	1. **Loop and iterator boundaries**: a same-language region with an
35	   iterator inside it has dynamic re-spawning. Is the iterator
36	   merged in (a loop in the generated code) or does it remain a
37	   spec-mediated boundary? If the latter, the merge buys less.
38	2. **Compile time**: whole-program optimization is slow. Does the
39	   incremental-compile model (issue 309's mtime check) still apply,
40	   or do edits require a full rebuild of the merged region?
41	3. **Debug story**: a stack trace inside the merged region needs to
42	   point back to a specific box. Either source-map-style mapping or
43	   per-function preserved names — both have costs.
44	
45	The right way to answer these is to prototype: take a small all-Lua
46	graph (like the hello map) and see what a merged version looks
47	like, both for runtime cost and for build-system complexity. The
48	prototype should be able to fall back to the per-box path if the
49	merge fails for any reason.
50	
51	## Why blocked until rest of phase 3 completes
52	
53	The merge optimization piggybacks on the per-box model — same
54	specs, same compile callback, same dispatch layer for cross-language
55	wires. Building it before the per-box model is settled risks
56	designing against a target that's still moving. After phase 3 ships
57	and the per-box path is stable, the merge becomes a clean
58	"everything except spec invocation gets shorter for this region."
59	
60	It's also genuinely lower priority than getting the runtime working
61	at all. The same-language fast path (issue 312) captures the
62	~2x JSON-skip win without any of this complexity. The merge wins a
63	further factor only if profile data shows function-call overhead
64	matters at all after 312 ships.
65	
66	## Scope of the research output
67	
68	The deliverable is a written design (not code) with:
69	- Concrete answers to the three questions above.
70	- A worked example: the merged C generated from a 5-box C-only
71	  graph, showing what the build artifact looks like.
72	- Estimated runtime cost of the per-box path vs the merged path
73	  on that example.
74	- A recommendation: ship as a feature, ship as an opt-in flag, or
75	  shelve indefinitely.
76	
77	After the design is reviewed, a separate implementation issue
78	opens (or doesn't, depending on the recommendation).
79	
80	## Relevant files
81	
82	- `issues/312-same-language-wire-fast-path.md` — the immediate JSON-
83	  skip win this builds on
84	- `issues/307-c-language-spec.md` — `compile` callback that would
85	  grow whole-program-aware semantics
86	- `issues/306-lua-language-spec.md` — Lua-side bytecode pre-compile
87	- `issues/309-build-system.md` — how the merge integrates with the
88	  compile/package step
89	
90	## Open questions (for the research, not for now)
91	
92	- Cross-language wires inside the merged region: the merged region
93	  still has cross-language outputs that exit to other regions. The
94	  spec interface still applies at those boundaries, so the merge is
95	  region-internal optimization, not a global rearchitecture.
96	- C ABI for inlined wrappers: with whole-program optimization the
97	  wrapper-per-box generation might collapse to nothing. If the
98	  optimizer can prove a wire endpoint is always called from the
99	  merged region with known types, it can skip the wrapper.
100	- Iterators-with-side-effects: an iterator self-rescheduling its
101	  own task is normally dispatch-layer machinery. Inside a merged
102	  region, it could become a `while` loop. Whether that's safe
103	  depends on whether other tasks are blocked on the iterator's
104	  output slots — which they shouldn't be in a same-language region,
105	  but the analysis has to confirm.
106	
```

### Tool result

```
1	# SoraMech — IPC and Threading Model
2	
3	## The problem
4	
5	The current executor runs boxes sequentially. Each box invocation shells out to
6	a driver script, which starts a new process, runs the function, and exits. The
7	return value passes through stdout. This works but has two structural problems:
8	
9	1. **Process-per-call cost.** Spawning a process takes roughly 1–10 ms depending
10	   on the runtime. For a map with ten boxes, that's dominated by spawn overhead,
11	   not by the actual work.
12	
13	2. **stdout is the wrong channel.** A box function that prints debug output
14	   corrupts the wire value. Values are transient — they vanish once the pipe is
15	   drained. The channel has no addressing, no framing, and no backpressure.
16	
17	The goal is a design where cross-language calls are fast, values are addressable,
18	and the execution model is ready to parallelize without redesign.
19	
20	---
21	
22	## The threading roadmap
23	
24	Three stages, each building on the last:
25	
26	**Stage 1 — Synchronous (current).** A single-threaded ready-queue walks the
27	graph in dependency order. `run_task(box, inputs)` in `src/004-executor.lua` is
28	the task boundary. It blocks until the driver responds.
29	
30	**Stage 2 — Coroutine-based non-blocking.** The ready-queue becomes a coroutine
31	scheduler. Boxes yield when waiting on upstream results or cross-language calls.
32	LuaSocket non-blocking I/O lets the scheduler run other boxes while one is
33	waiting for a response. No true parallelism yet, but the graph's natural
34	concurrency is exploited without blocking the whole process.
35	
36	**Stage 3 — SoraMech-owned thread pool.** A pool implementation
37	written for SoraMech, with the 3d-rts pool design as a reference.
38	The `task_fn` boundary swaps in the thread pool. Worker threads pull
39	tasks from the pool; OS-level scheduling replaces any single-threaded
40	ordering. Cross-language calls block a worker thread while other
41	threads continue. True parallelism across cores.
42	
43	Effil-jit is not used. Coroutines are not used. The path goes
44	straight from synchronous (phase 2) to the C pool (phase 3) — no
45	intermediate coroutine stage.
46	
47	---
48	
49	## Stage 3 detail: pthreads task model
50	
51	Each box execution in the thread pool is a **task struct** containing:
52	
53	- **Function pointer** — the language-specific invocation wrapper (a C
54	  function, since pthreads requires a C entry point)
55	- **Shared memory pointer** — the region holding the input arguments and
56	  return value for this invocation
57	- **Dependency list** — pointers to the tasks this task is waiting on
58	- **Waiting list** — pointers to tasks that are waiting on this task
59	- **Counter** (iterator boxes only) — current iteration index, updated
60	  in-place when the iterator re-queues itself
61	
62	Task structs are allocated once at run start, one per box (not one per
63	invocation). They live for the entire run.
64	
65	### Blocking and unblocking — pointer-only transitions
66	
67	When a task cannot run (upstream output not yet available), it is placed
68	in its upstream task's waiting list. This is a pointer move — no
69	allocation, no struct copy. The task struct stays exactly where it is.
70	
71	When an upstream task completes and writes its return value to shared
72	memory, it walks its waiting list and re-adds each entry to the main task
73	queue. Again, a pointer move only. The unblocked task's struct is
74	unchanged; only its position in the queue changes.
75	
76	This means "blocked", "queued", and "running" are states defined by which
77	list a task's pointer currently lives in — not by any field in the struct.
78	
79	### Per-thread language runtimes
80	
81	Each worker thread initializes its own language runtimes at startup:
82	- **Lua:** one `lua_State` per thread. Lua states are not thread-safe, but
83	  one per thread requires no locking. The task wrapper calls the box
84	  function via `lua_pcall`.
85	- **C:** the compiled box function is called directly through the function
86	  pointer. No interpreter, no overhead beyond the call itself.
87	- **Bash/other:** a persistent subprocess per thread (Unix domain socket
88	  server model — see Option 3 above). The task wrapper sends a request and
89	  blocks the thread waiting for the response.
90	
91	### Iterator self-re-queue
92	
93	An iterator task does not terminate after one invocation. When it
94	completes:
95	1. It increments its `counter` field in-place (or wraps to 0).
96	2. It checks its input queue for the next pending value.
97	3. If a value is waiting: it updates its input pointer and re-adds itself
98	   to the main task queue. No allocation — the same task struct re-enters
99	   the queue with updated state.
100	4. If no value is waiting: it adds itself to the upstream task's waiting
101	   list, to be re-added when the next input arrives.
102	
103	Input values are processed one-at-a-time in arrival order. Ten queued
104	inputs means the iterator task visits the queue ten times, advancing the
105	counter and firing a different output path each time.
106	
107	### Blocking semantics for long-running operations
108	
109	The pool's parallelism comes from running N worker threads concurrently
110	on N cores. Each worker is sitting inside a single dispatch task at any
111	given moment. If a box function does something that takes a long time —
112	`sleep`, blocking I/O, a synchronous network request, waiting on a long-
113	running subprocess — **the worker thread is occupied for the full
114	duration**. It cannot pick up other tasks while it waits.
115	
116	Concretely:
117	- 16-worker pool, 1 box sleeping 10 seconds → the other 15 workers
118	  continue normally.
119	- 16-worker pool, 16 boxes all sleeping → all workers parked, no other
120	  task runs until a sleep finishes.
121	- The pool size is also the budget for "blocking activities". Heavy
122	  use of `sleep`-style boxes means raising `SORAMECH_WORKERS`.
123	
124	Why the runtime does not have a scheduler: SoraMech is deliberately not
125	in the business of "park this task and wake it at time T." There is no
126	delayed-spawn primitive, no timer thread, no polling loop. Adding one
127	would mean a second concurrency model alongside the pool, with its own
128	synchronization story. Instead, we lean on the pool's existing
129	mechanism: a task either runs (occupying a worker) or is blocked on a
130	slot waiting for a value (parked, no worker held). Long sleeps are an
131	"occupying a worker" case.
132	
133	For users who need many concurrent waits without burning workers, the
134	graph-level pattern is cooperative: split the operation into "kick off
135	the thing" (returns immediately) and "check whether the thing is done"
136	(returns immediately, returns a status), and wire them through an
137	iterator with a queued input. The iterator polls. No individual box
138	blocks for long. Each box completes quickly, freeing its worker.
139	
140	This is a documented constraint, not a bug. Box authors should know
141	that an unbounded `sleep` inside a function is the equivalent of
142	holding a worker hostage. The cooperative pattern is the way around
143	it.
144	
145	---
146	
147	## The three IPC options
148	
149	### Option 1 — LuaJIT FFI (Lua ↔ C, in-process)
150	
151	The Foreign Function Interface lets Lua call C functions that live in a shared
152	library (`.so`) directly, in the same process, with no subprocess and no channel.
153	You provide a C declaration (`ffi.cdef[[ int add(int a, int b); ]]`), load the
154	compiled library (`ffi.load("./mybox.so")`), and call `lib.add(3, 4)` — the CPU
155	jumps into native code and returns. Overhead is nanoseconds.
156	
157	Since the executor already does mtime-based dynamic compilation (see `drivers/c.sh`),
158	compiling to a `.so` instead of a binary is just a flag change (`-shared -fPIC`).
159	The box function signature changes from `main(argc, argv)` to a named function
160	with typed arguments — which is strictly cleaner.
161	
162	**Limitation:** FFI only works for languages that compile to a C-compatible ABI.
163	Bash cannot. Python can (via `ctypes` on the other side), but it's awkward.
164	FFI is the right answer for Lua ↔ C only.
165	
166	**Cross-platform:** LuaJIT FFI works on Linux, macOS, and Windows. `.so` becomes
167	`.dylib` on macOS and `.dll` on Windows — the `ffi.load()` call is portable if
168	you handle the extension.
169	
170	---
171	
172	### Option 2 — Shared memory (`shm_open` + `mmap`)
173	
174	`shm_open("/soramech-wire-0", ...)` creates a named memory segment managed by
175	the kernel — not a file on disk, but a named object in RAM. `mmap()` maps it
176	into the calling process's address space. Any other process that opens the same
177	name gets access to the *same physical memory*. A write in one process is
178	immediately visible in another — no copy, no syscall per access after setup.
179	
180	For SoraMech, each wire between two boxes would correspond to a named shared
181	memory slot. The compiler (issue 219) generates the slot names and the read/write
182	calls. Any language that can call POSIX can participate:
183	
184	- Lua: via LuaJIT FFI calling `shm_open` + `mmap` from libc
185	- C: natively
186	- Bash: via `/dev/shm/<name>` — on Linux, the tmpfs backing shared memory is
187	  exposed as a filesystem path, so bash can `cat /dev/shm/soramech-wire-0`
188	
189	**Performance:** After the initial `mmap()` setup, reads and writes are pointer
190	dereferences — no syscalls, no copies. For small values (strings, numbers) this
191	is essentially free.
192	
193	**Complication:** Shared memory is untyped raw bytes. You need a size header
194	(how many bytes is this value?), a type tag (string? number? JSON object?), and
195	a synchronization mechanism (how does the reader know the writer is done?). None
196	of these are hard — a small fixed header in the segment handles all three — but
197	the compiler must generate all of it correctly for every wire.
198	
199	**Cross-platform:**
200	- Linux: full support. `/dev/shm/` filesystem exposure is Linux-specific.
... [truncated; 200-line cap]
```

## Assistant

_Tools:_

- `Read: /mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech/docs/001-architecture.md`

### Tool result

```
1	# SoraMech — Architecture
2	
3	## The structural shell
4	
5	SoraMech is a structural shell. The thread pool, the slot store, the
6	dispatch layer, the allocator — these are the steel and concrete of
7	an apartment building. They hold the roof up. They do not decorate
8	the rooms.
9	
10	Box authors design the rooms. Library authors furnish them. The
11	shell provides the framing, the load-bearing walls, the structural
12	guarantees of what can hang where. The shell does not know what
13	colour the curtains are.
14	
15	> "We are creating a structural shell, like an apartment building
16	> that is just steel and concrete at first, and the user can build
17	> the walls and windows and design the doors and movement paths as
18	> they please. The library authors are doing the actual decorating,
19	> with tables and chairs and pictures of the beach. We are
20	> structural, we keep the roof from caving in. To that end, we must
21	> be rigid. We must be firm. We must be flexible." — 2026-05-20
22	
23	Two consequences shape every design decision below.
24	
25	### One allocator function per kind of operation
26	
27	For each distinct kind of allocation the runtime needs, exactly one
28	function performs it. The same function is called from every box's
29	hot path; only its parameters vary. The user's code, the spec's
30	code, the dispatch layer's code — all reach the same entry point.
31	
32	This is the rigidity. The shell does not present "convenient
33	shortcuts" for special cases. Every value lifecycle goes through
34	the same allocator. Every task struct comes from the same slab.
35	Every wire push goes through the same `slot_push`. No alternatives,
36	no escape hatches.
37	
38	The exceptions are setup and teardown: the graph loader allocates
39	every box's slot table once at startup before the pool launches;
40	`pool_destroy` releases everything in one shot at the end. Outside
41	those two moments, the shell is uniform.
42	
43	### No single-threaded process outside the pool
44	
45	Every piece of runtime work — every allocation, every reference
46	count update, every dispatch action, every cleanup — happens
47	inside a pool task. There is no background garbage collector, no
48	maintenance daemon, no separate scheduler. The pool is the
49	universal scheduler; the pool task is the universal unit of work.
50	
51	Consequences:
52	
53	- The allocator's deep sweep, when it runs, runs inside the
54	  failing-to-allocate task. There is no "sweep thread" polling for
55	  free memory. There is no quiescence-triggered sweep either — we
56	  cannot assume there will ever be a quiescent moment in a graph
57	  with self-feeding iterators.
58	- Reference count decrements happen at the end of the task that
59	  was holding the reference. Cleanup is the task's last act, not
60	  a separate worker's job.
61	- Box-level retirement (when all of a box's possible inputs are
62	  exhausted) is checked inside the task whose completion may have
63	  triggered the retirement condition.
64	
65	The pool's parallelism is the only parallelism the runtime
66	provides. A side thread would be a different shape — a violation
67	of the uniform "one allocator, one scheduler" rule.
68	
69	## Three programs, one data format
70	
71	SoraMech is three independent programs that share a common on-disk
72	format: the map directory. None of the three knows about the others
73	at runtime.
74	
75	```
76	  Browser (assets/index.html)
77	       |  HTTP (file CRUD)
78	       v
79	  src/006-server-main.lua       [map directory]
80	                                maps/<name>/
81	                                  meta.json
82	                                  boxes/
83	                                  data/
84	                                  src/
85	                                  compiled/   (after compile button)
86	                                  tmp/ -> /tmp/<name>/
87	       ^
88	       | reads & writes
89	  Runner (one of):
90	    src/007-runner-main.lua     ← phase 2, synchronous Lua interpreter
91	    soramech-pool (binary)      ← phase 3, C thread pool runner
92	```
93	
94	### src/006-server-main.lua (the editor's HTTP backend)
95	
96	Thin file CRUD proxy. Receives JSON requests from the browser and
97	translates them into reads and writes on the map directory. No logic
98	beyond path validation and basic consistency checks (e.g. reject a
99	delete if the box is still referenced by another box's connection
100	list).
101	
102	Started with: `luajit src/006-server-main.lua [port]` (default 7700).
103	The `run` script at the project root starts the server and opens the
104	editor.
105	
106	The editor is editor-only — no run button, no run results display.
107	The runner is invoked separately, by command line. `last-run.jsonl`
108	is the runner's output, consumed by external tooling, not by the
109	editor.
110	
111	### Phase 2 runner — `src/007-runner-main.lua`
112	
113	Synchronous Lua interpreter. Loads a map directory, validates the
114	graph, walks boxes in dependency order, invokes language drivers per
115	box (`drivers/lua.sh`, `drivers/c.sh`, `drivers/bash.sh`). Each box
116	invocation is wrapped in a `task_fn(inputs) -> outputs` boundary that
117	matches the phase 3 thread pool's action signature.
118	
119	This is the development target while the editor is being built out.
120	Phase 2 retires wholesale when phase 3 lands; the editor and the
121	map-directory format are unchanged across the cutover.
122	
123	### Phase 3 runner — `soramech-pool` (`src/008-pool-runner.c`)
124	
125	The phase 3 runner is a single C binary. It owns:
126	
127	- A **SoraMech-built thread pool** under `libs/task-pool/` (3d-rts at
128	  `/home/ritz/programming/ai-stuff/games/3d-rts/libs/900-task-pool.h`
129	  is the design reference, not vendored).
130	- A **C graph loader** (`src/graph-loader.c`) — replaces
131	  `src/003-loader.lua`. The runner has no embedded Lua state at the
132	  top level; Lua only appears inside a Lua language spec's per-worker
133	  handle.
134	- A **per-input-port slot store** (`src/slot-store.c`) — ring-buffer
135	  cells in process heap, durable for the run, plus a large-value heap
136	  for variable-size payloads.
137	- A **dispatch layer** (`src/dispatch.c`) — the single worker-side
138	  action: read inputs → invoke spec → pick branch → push outputs.
139	- A **language spec registry** (`src/spec-registry.c`) — `dlopen`s
140	  every `langs/<name>/spec.so` at startup and registers it by name
141	  and file extension.
142	- A **run-output writer** — a dedicated thread that drains a
143	  multi-producer-single-consumer ring of events and appends one JSON
144	  line per event to `tmp/last-run.jsonl`.
145	
146	One pool per map run. Created after the graph loads and validates;
147	destroyed after the active-task counter reaches zero. Worker count
148	defaults to logical CPUs (capped at `MAX_WORKERS=16`), overridable
149	via `SORAMECH_WORKERS=N`.
150	
151	See "Execution model — phase 3" below for the action loop and slot
152	mechanics.
153	
154	### assets/index.html (the editor)
155	
156	Static HTML + vanilla JS. Infinite-scroll canvas. Box diagram editor.
157	Talks to `src/006-server-main.lua` for all file operations. No run
158	button. The Compile button (added in phase 2 as UI only) wires into
159	the phase 3 build/package pipeline once it lands.
160	
161	## Map directory format
162	
163	```
164	maps/<name>/
165	  meta.json          — { name, description, src_dirs?, lang? }
166	  boxes/
167	    <id>.json        — one file per box (see Box file format below)
168	  data/
169	    <name>.json      — soramech-data tables; section-level "constant" flag
170	  src/
171	    *.lua, *.c, *.sh — box function source files
172	  compiled/          — produced by the compile pipeline (issue 309)
173	    pool-runner      — copy or symlink of soramech-pool
174	    src/             — every source file the map uses
175	    bin/             — compiled .so files (C boxes via the C spec's compile callback)
176	    langs/<name>/    — copy of each language spec .so the map uses
177	    manifest.json    — every box, its language, its artifact path, build version
178	  tmp/               — symlink to /tmp/<name>/
179	    last-run.jsonl   — phase 3 run log (JSON Lines)
180	    logs/            — additional run logs
181	```
182	
183	A compiled map runs standalone: `./compiled/pool-runner` looks up its
184	specs in `compiled/langs/`, reads `manifest.json`, and executes
185	without any reference to the live editor source tree.
186	
187	Phase 2's `drivers.json` file is gone. Phase 2 resolves drivers by
188	file extension via the `drivers/` directory; phase 3 resolves
189	languages by file extension via the spec registry (each spec declares
190	its `file_ext`). No map-local driver config file is needed in either
191	phase.
192	
193	The `data/` directory is for `soramech-data` storage (see "Data
194	files" below). It is **not** where file-source boxes read from — those

But the bigger question your description raises: are we talking about **runtime per-edge classification** (slot store carries either native bytes or JSON bytes per wire, dispatch reads the per-edge bit each box invocation), or **compile-time per-language concatenation** (all the Lua source for the map gets merged into one Lua file at compile, all the C source into one .c — same-language hops become direct function calls in the concatenated output, never touching the slot store)?

The first is 312 as currently written. The second is 313 (whole-program same-language merge) — which is marked research / blocked. Your wording — "all the C files go into the same C file, all the Lua files go into the same Lua file, etc. Then, it's executed by the C graph loader thread pool system" — sounds like the second.

The two are very different code paths:

- **Per-edge runtime classification (312)**: slot store still mediates same-lang wires. The bytes in the slot are language-native (Lua's `lua_dump` output, or msgpack, or whatever the Lua spec picks). The spec's `invoke_native` reads/writes those bytes. The win is "skip JSON encode/decode" — but you still serialize, hand bytes through the slot, and deserialize.
- **Compile-time concatenation (313)**: same-lang wires don't exist as runtime entities at all. The producer's return value lives on the consumer's Lua stack already, because both are functions in the same `lua_State`. No slot, no serialize, no spec invocation. The slot store and dispatch layer only kick in at language borders.

```
1	---
2	name: Cross-language wire format
3	description: How values cross language boundaries in SoraMech — JSON-always with shared-memory $ref primitives, no handles, no back-routing
4	type: project
5	originSessionId: e9ed70c7-2b63-425c-987c-6431040bedfb
6	---
7	The cross-language data passing design, validated 2026-05-19.
8	
9	**Wire format is JSON always.** Every spec exposes only `to_json(native_value, output_signature)` and `from_json(json, input_signature)`. Specs never know about other languages — adding a new language means implementing those two functions. No pair-wise paths (no "lua → c" code path); the language-agnostic JSON intermediate is non-negotiable.
10	
11	**JSON can carry shared-memory references.** A primitive like `{"$ref": {"ptr": 140234..., "len": 1048576, "type": "bytes"}}` references bytes living in shared heap. In-process specs (Lua, C) deref directly or copy per their language's needs (`lua_pushlstring` copies into Lua's heap; C reads at the pointer). Cross-process boundaries (anything ↔ Bash) inline the bytes at the UDS marshaller. The wire format itself is always JSON; `$ref` is just one primitive form.
12	
13	**No handles, no back-routing.** A "handle that routes operations back to the owning spec" was rejected — real library functions (qsort, libcurl, etc.) can't interpret SoraMech ids. Destination spec either has the means to interpret what arrived (e.g. Lua's spec uses LuaJIT FFI to wrap a `$function_pointer` primitive into a callable) or the wire compile fails. Specs are self-contained.
14	
15	**Type ambiguities resolved by wiring, not annotation.** The system never asks vocab questions like "is param 2 the discriminator?" or "is this int the length?" — it can't, because that vocabulary isn't universal. A C function `void foo(char *p, int n)` exposes two independent input slots; the user wires them according to the function's documentation. Same for unions (one slot for the variant bytes, one slot for the tag). The system never infers pairings; the user expresses them by wiring.
16	
17	**Spec interface (final shape):**
18	- `to_json(native_value, output_signature) → json` — emits JSON with embedded `$ref` / `$function_pointer` / `$lang_opaque` primitives as needed.
19	- `from_json(json, input_signature) → native_value` — reconstructs the value as the destination language's native form. May synthesize callables (libffi trampolines for C, `ffi.cast` for LuaJIT) when the destination's signature is a function-pointer type.
20	- `list_functions(path)` and `infer_signature(path, fn)` — editor introspection. All function parameters exposed as separate slots; no inference about pairings.
21	- plus existing `init`, `call`, `shutdown`.
22	
23	**Wire primitives in the JSON contract:**
24	- `$ref` — pointer + length + type tag for shared-memory bytes. In-process only; marshaller inlines bytes at cross-process boundary.
25	- `$function_pointer` — address + signature for callables. In-process only; wire compile fails into Bash.
26	- `$lang_opaque` — tag saying "these bytes are language-X-internal." Destination spec either knows what to do or wire compile fails. Used for things like Lua coroutines into C where C has no general way to interpret the bytes.
27	
28	**Why:** Three corrections drove the design:
29	1. Handles were rejected because library functions in the wild can't be modified to use them.
30	2. Pair-wise paths were rejected because every new language would break the system.
31	3. Per-function discriminator/length annotations were rejected because the system has no vocabulary to ask about them universally.
32	
33	**How to apply:** When discussing cross-language data passing, spec design, the wire format, or the JSON bridge: every wire carries JSON, period. Big values use `$ref` primitives that reference shared heap. Specs implement only the two pair-symmetric callbacks. Type ambiguities are user wiring decisions, not system prompts. The natural home for this design in the issue tree is issue 317; the C-side parser/wrapper work is issue 307.
34	
```

# 309 — Build system & Makefile orchestration

## Status
complete (2026-05-20) — top-level Makefile, per-language spec
Makefiles, vendored sources under `libs/task-pool/` and
`libs/json/`, spec discovery via `/proc/self/exe`, and
`scripts/soramech-compile.sh` producing a portable `compiled/`
directory all in place. Verified end-to-end via the
"compile pipeline (portable run)" integration check.

## Current behavior
Phase 2 has no compiled binary. The runner is a Lua script
(`src/007-runner-main.lua`) invoked via `luajit`. Drivers are shell
scripts that compile per-box C source on demand. There is no
top-level Makefile, no vendoring of C dependencies, no link step
beyond per-driver compile.

Phase 3 introduces a real C binary (the pool runner) plus a set of
language spec `.so` files. They need to compile in the right order
with the right flags, and the build needs to be reproducible from a
clean checkout.

## Concept

A top-level `Makefile` at the project root orchestrates the entire
phase 3 build. It builds:

1. The pool runner binary: `soramech-pool` (statically links the
   SoraMech-owned task pool, the vendored JSON parser, the slot
   store, the graph loader, the dispatch layer, the spec registry).
2. Each language spec: `langs/<name>/spec.so`, built by a small
   per-spec Makefile.
3. (Optional) Unit test binaries.

Each component has its own Makefile if it has non-trivial build
rules. The top-level Makefile delegates to them via `$(MAKE) -C
<dir>`. A clean checkout becomes a working build with `make`.

## Directory layout

```
soramech/
├── Makefile                       ← top-level orchestration
├── soramech-pool                  ← built binary (gitignored)
├── src/
│   ├── 008-pool-runner.c          ← main entry, pool lifecycle (issue 301)
│   ├── slot-store.c / .h          ← per-task slot allocator (issue 302)
│   ├── graph-loader.c / .h        ← C loader, replaces 003-loader.lua (issue 305)
│   ├── dispatch.c / .h            ← dispatch action (issue 304)
│   └── spec-registry.c / .h       ← spec dlopen/registry (issue 303)
├── libs/
│   ├── task-pool/                 ← SoraMech-built thread pool
│   │   ├── pool.h
│   │   └── pool.c
│   └── json/                      ← vendored JSON parser (cJSON, jsmn, etc.)
│       ├── cjson.h
│       └── cjson.c
├── langs/
│   ├── lua/
│   │   ├── Makefile               ← builds spec.so
```

325	static int read_inputs(const dispatch_ctx_t *ctx, const box_t *b,
326	                       char **bufs, ua_chunk_t **buf_chunks,
327	                       const void **datas, int *sizes,
328	                       int per_buf_cap, int *failed)
329	{
330	    *failed = 0;
331	    if (b->n_inputs == 0 || !b->input_slot_ids) return 0;
332	    ua_t *heap = slot_store_allocator(ctx->slots);
333	    int n_present = 0;
334	    for (int i = 0; i < b->n_inputs; i++) {
335	        buf_chunks[i] = ua_alloc(heap, (size_t)per_buf_cap);
336	        if (!buf_chunks[i]) { *failed = 1; return n_present; }
337	        bufs[i] = ua_data(buf_chunks[i]);
338	        int got;

342	                           bufs[i], per_buf_cap);
343	        } else {
344	            got = slot_peek(ctx->slots, b->input_slot_ids[i],
345	                            bufs[i], per_buf_cap);

347	        if (got < 0) {

351	            if (!b->inputs[i].optional) { *failed = 1; return n_present; }
352	            continue;

354	        datas[n_present] = bufs[i];
355	        sizes[n_present] = got;
