71	
72	/* {{{ c_compile() — gcc -shared -fPIC */
73	static int c_compile(const char *src_path, const char *out_path)
74	{
75	    if (!src_path || !out_path) return -1;
76	    pid_t pid = fork();
77	    if (pid < 0) return -1;
78	    if (pid == 0) {
79	        /* Child: replace ourselves with gcc. */
80	        execlp("gcc", "gcc",
81	               "-shared", "-fPIC", "-O2", "-Wall",
82	               "-o", out_path, src_path, (char *)NULL);
83	        _exit(127);
84	    }
85	    int status = 0;
86	    if (waitpid(pid, &status, 0) < 0) return -1;
87	    if (!WIFEXITED(status) || WEXITSTATUS(status) != 0) {
88	        fprintf(stderr, "c spec: gcc failed for '%s' → '%s' (status=%d)\n",
89	                src_path, out_path, status);
90	        return -1;
91	    }
92	    return 0;
93	}
94	/* }}} */
95	
96	/* {{{ ends_in_dot_c() */
97	static int ends_in_dot_c(const char *p)
98	{
99	    size_t n = strlen(p);
100	    return n >= 2 && p[n - 2] == '.' && p[n - 1] == 'c';
101	}
102	/* }}} */
103	
104	/* {{{ hash_path() — small DJB2-like hash for deriving cache filenames */
105	static unsigned long hash_path(const char *p)
106	{
107	    unsigned long h = 5381;
108	    for (; *p; p++) h = h * 33u + (unsigned char)*p;
109	    return h;
110	}
111	/* }}} */
112	
113	/* {{{ maybe_lazy_compile() — if file_path is a .c source, compile it
114	 * to a /tmp .so and return that path; otherwise return file_path
115	 * unchanged. The output buffer is the caller-supplied so_buf. */
116	static const char *maybe_lazy_compile(const char *file_path,
117	                                      char *so_buf, size_t so_cap)
118	{
119	    if (!ends_in_dot_c(file_path)) return file_path;
120	    /* Hash the source path so repeat opens of the same source file
121	     * pick up the same .so. Concurrent invokes will race on gcc but
122	     * the inputs are identical, so the output converges. */
123	    unsigned long h = hash_path(file_path);
124	    int n = snprintf(so_buf, so_cap, "/tmp/soramech-c-%lx.so", h);
125	    if (n < 0 || (size_t)n >= so_cap) return NULL;

## Assistant

Plain-English recap:

**What changed:**

Each C box can now declare in its JSON which extra compiler flags it wants and which libraries to link against. Three new optional fields:

- `cflags`: extra flags passed straight to the compiler. Useful for `-DSOMETHING`, `-O0 -g`, `-mavx2`, anything the user normally hands to `gcc`.
- `link_libs`: an array of library names. Each becomes a `-lname` flag automatically. So `["m", "curl"]` links the math library and libcurl.
- `headers`: an array of header names. Parsed but not yet used — these light up when the C spec grows its typed-wrapper generation (the deferred item that lets a Lua box hand a table to a C box and have it arrive as a typed struct).

The spec interface itself grew a third parameter on its compile callback so the C spec can actually see the box record (and read those new fields). Lua and Bash specs don't have a compile callback so they're unaffected. The internal "raw .c file passed at invoke time" path passes `NULL` for the box — it doesn't have a box record to point at, just a file path.

**Two new tests prove it works end-to-end:**

- A small C source whose behavior depends on `#ifdef SORAMECH_FLAG_ON` — built once without the flag (returns "off"), once with `cflags="-DSORAMECH_FLAG_ON"` (returns "on"). Proves the flag string reaches the compiler.
- A C source that calls `sqrt(16.0)` from the math library — built with `link_libs=["m"]` (`-lm`), invoked, returns "4". Proves the `-l<name>` translation works through the gcc invocation.

**Test suite:** all green. 22 + 14 + 5 + 7 + 3 + 4 + 8 + 13 + 11 + 2 + 9 + **10** (C-spec, up from 8) + 5 + 22 + 5 + 11 = passing.

**Remaining task list across the issues:**

- `issue 307` item 2 — JSON output writing from C (needs typed wrappers); the `headers` field also goes live then.
- `issue 312` — the mixed-fan-out same-language consumer awkward case (only a deeper contract change fixes it).
- `issue 318` — sentinel primitives implementation.
- `issue 308` — Bash side equivalents (small, mostly cosmetic).
- `issue 313` — C-side concatenation, Bash sourcing, dispatch inlining.

Picking the next one — the sentinel primitives (`issue 318`) feel like the highest-impact next step, since they're what unblock function pointers and shared-memory handles across language boundaries. Or 313's C-side concatenation if you'd rather keep the merge work going. Pick?

## Assistant

Switching modes — let me walk through the design choices before diving in.

**The thing that's been blocking 307 item 2:**

The C spec's invoke doesn't currently know what shape the user said the box returns. The box JSON declares `returns: "int"` or `returns: "string"` etc., but that information lives on the box record in the graph loader. The dispatch knows it, the spec doesn't.

For the C spec to write _real_ JSON on cross-language outputs (the symmetric of what Lua already does), it needs to look at that declared return type and pick the JSON shape: a `returns: "string"` should produce `"hello"` (with quotes and escaping), a `returns: "int"` should produce `42` (raw digits, which is valid JSON), and so on.

**There are two ways to give the spec that information:**

1. **Extend the invoke signature with `const box_t *box`** — symmetric with what we just did to compile, gives the spec full access to the box record, future-proof for things like sentinel primitives that need more box context.
2. **Add a single `output_type` string parameter to invoke** — narrower, less invasive, but if we ever need anything else from the box record we're back here.

Option 1 is the same flavor of cross-cutting change we did to compile, and it composes — once invoke has `box_t *`, sentinel primitives in 318 can use box->returns to decide which sentinel kind to emit, the typed-wrapper generation eventually uses it for input projection too. Option 2 closes a door we keep needing to reopen.

I'm going to go with option 1. Here's the question I want you to think about while I do the mechanical part:

**Question for the duck:** the C spec's "wrap the bytes as JSON per declared type" needs to handle `returns: "string"` specially (wrap + escape). For the other primitive types (`int`, `double`, `bool`), the C function's raw output is _already_ valid JSON — `42` is a JSON number, `true` is a JSON bool. So the C spec only really has work to do for strings and (future) tables. Does that match how you've been thinking about it? Or did you want stricter validation — make the C spec _check_ that the bytes match the declared type before passing them through?

I'll start the work. Coming back to your answer.

## Assistant

**Concept check before moving on.** Now that the spec sees the box record at invoke time, it can read whatever fields the graph loader populated. The next step is making sure the `returns` field actually exists in `box_t` (right now boxes have `inputs` but no symmetric `returns`). 

A tiny design note worth pausing on: a box's `inputs` is an array of port declarations (because a box can have many input ports), but `returns` is a single value (because every box has exactly one output wire — that's the project's "single output wire" rule from issue 218). So we don't need an array — just one string for the declared return type.

## Assistant

**Now the C spec's JSON-output branch.** Conceptually, the spec writes its bytes _twice_-conditionally:

- `output_native = 1` → write the user function's raw bytes (existing behavior; the same-language fast path).
- `output_native = 0` → wrap based on `box->returns`:
  - `"string"` → JSON-quoted with escape sequences for `"`, `\`, control characters.
  - `"int"` / `"double"` / `"bool"` / `"null"` / `"json"` → raw passthrough (already valid JSON).
  - `"void"` → empty.
  - missing / unknown → raw passthrough (safest fallback).

JSON string escaping is straightforward but easy to get wrong. The `libs/json` library has a writer that handles escaping — `json_writer_string` writes a properly-escaped JSON string. I'll use it instead of hand-rolling.
